./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-2-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-3-**Status:** Proposed — 2026-08-21
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-4-**Deciders:** operator (jedarden)
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:5:**Tracking:** plan.md §18; motivated by the 2026-08-21 fleet-wide OTLP migration
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-6-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-7-## Context
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-8-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-9-On 2026-08-21 the ex44 fleet was migrated to OTLP export. The change that had
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:10:to take effect was a single boolean — `telemetry.otlp_sink.enabled` in
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-11-`~/.config/needle/config.yaml`. Applying it required draining and relaunching
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-12-**all fifteen workers**, a rollover that took ~55 minutes of wall clock and was
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-13-bounded not by the work but by however long each worker's in-flight agent
--
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-34-1. **The cost of a config change is a fleet rollover.** Not a restart of one
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-35-   process — a staged drain of every worker, each releasing a claimed bead.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-36-2. **A config flip is *armed*, not inert.** needle ≥0.4 fails closed when
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:37:   `otlp_sink.enabled` is true and `NEEDLE_OTLP_AUTHORIZATION` is absent:
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-38-   `init_tracing_subscriber` → `build_http_providers()?` propagates and the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-39-   worker refuses to boot. Editing the file while a header-less fleet runs does
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-40-   not do nothing — it means the *next* restart of each worker kills it.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-41-3. **Config that cannot apply is silently discarded.** `telemetry` sits in
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:42:   `NON_OVERRIDABLE_KEYS`, so `telemetry.otlp_sink.enabled: true` in a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-43-   workspace `.needle.yaml` is parsed, warned to a log nobody reads, and
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-44-   dropped. The fleet ran for days with a config file that looked correct.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-45-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:46:The seam this needs already exists: `check_hot_reload()` runs after the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-47-`LOGGING` state, described in-code as running "between dispatch cycles, never
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:48:mid-claim, ensuring no bead is left in_progress." Configuration reload is the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-49-same problem with a smaller blast radius.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-50-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-51-**But that seam's own history is a warning, not a reassurance.**
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:52:`check_hot_reload` carried `#[allow(dead_code)]` and had **no call site
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-53-anywhere** from its introduction on 2026-03-21 until it was wired in around
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-54-2026-08-16 (`needle-eea03800`) — for five months, while its doc comment stated
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-55-it was "called between LOGGING and SELECTING." Every deploy in that window was
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:56:a manual kill-and-relaunch that people believed was a hot-reload. It is
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-57-genuinely live now (21 `binary.freshness.exit` events as of 2026-08-21), so the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-58-boundary is real and exercised — but it has days of production evidence, not
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-59-months.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-60-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:61:The lesson transfers directly: a reload mechanism that is *documented* to run is
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:62:not a reload mechanism that runs. Phase 18 must prove the config check actually
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-63-executes, with a test that fails if the call site disappears — not inherit the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-64-assumption that wiring it once means it stays wired.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-65-
--
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-67-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-68-### 1. Reload is evaluated only at the existing cycle boundary
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-69-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:70:The reload check runs where `check_hot_reload()` runs — after `do_log()`,
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-71-between dispatch cycles, never mid-claim and never mid-dispatch. A worker in
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-72-`Building`/`Dispatching`/`Executing`/`Handling` holds a bead; nothing about its
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-73-configuration may change underneath it.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-74-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:75:**Rejected:** applying a reload the moment it is detected. It would let
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-76-`agent.timeout` or an adapter definition change while a dispatch that was
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-77-launched under the old value is still running, producing outcomes attributable
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-78-to neither config.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-79-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-80-### 2. The trigger is a polled mtime+hash check, interval-gated
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-81-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:82:Gated by a new `worker.config_reload_check_interval_secs` (`0` disables, and is
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:83:the default until the feature has fleet time). This mirrors `check_hot_reload`'s
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-84-existing binary-hash check exactly, and adds **no new dependency**.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-85-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-86-**Rejected — SIGHUP.** SIGHUP is already bound to the shutdown flag:
--
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-88-together, deliberately, because a killed tmux session delivers SIGHUP to the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-89-worker and the handler exists so the worker can release its bead and emit
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-90-`worker.stopped` instead of dying silently. Repurposing SIGHUP would turn every
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:91:tmux teardown into a reload. This constraint is not obvious from the outside and
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-92-is the single most likely wrong turn for an implementer here.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-93-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-94-**Rejected — a file watcher (`notify`).** A new dependency and a new async task,
--
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-96-defer to the cycle boundary regardless. The poll is strictly simpler for the
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-97-same delivered behaviour.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-98-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:99:### 3. Every config key has a declared reload tier
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-100-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-101-The tier is declared in code as a table, not inferred:
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-102-
--
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-107-| **C — immutable** | Requires a restart. | worker identity / `qualified_id`, `workspace.home`, `bead_cli.backend`, the tokio runtime, the shape of the tracing subscriber stack |
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-108-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-109-**Rejected:** a single atomic swap of the whole `Config` with no tiering. It
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:110:reads as if everything is reloadable, and would silently no-op for the Tier-C
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-111-keys — recreating the exact `NON_OVERRIDABLE_KEYS` failure that caused this
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-112-work.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-113-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:114:### 4. Validate before swap; a reload may never fail closed
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-115-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-116-The candidate config is validated with the existing `ConfigLoader::validate`
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-117-before anything is swapped. On any error the running config is kept, a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:118:`config.reload.rejected` event is emitted with the errors, and the worker
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-119-continues on its current configuration.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-120-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-121-This is non-negotiable and is the direct lesson of the OTLP header: a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-122-configuration problem must degrade telemetry, never remove a worker. A typo in a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:123:YAML file must not be able to take down the fleet — and with polled reload, a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-124-bad edit would otherwise reach every worker simultaneously within one interval,
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-125-which is *worse* than the restart-only status quo.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-126-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:127:### 5. The reload seam is installed unconditionally at boot
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-128-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:129:The OTLP tracing layer is wrapped in `tracing_subscriber::reload::Layer` from
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-130-the first moment of process start — **including when OTLP is disabled**, where
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-131-it wraps a no-op layer.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-132-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-133-This is the load-bearing decision. `try_init()` is one-shot: a process that
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:134:booted without a reload handle can never acquire one. If the seam is installed
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-135-only when OTLP is already enabled, then turning OTLP *on* still requires a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-136-restart — which is precisely the situation this ADR exists to eliminate, and it
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:137:would not be discovered until someone tried it on a live fleet. `reload` is not
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-138-feature-gated in the pinned `tracing-subscriber` 0.3.23, so this costs no new
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-139-dependency.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-140-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-141-### 6. Changes that cannot be applied are reported, never silently ignored
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-142-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:143:A Tier-C key changing produces a `config.reload.restart_required` event naming
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-144-the keys, and a WARN. The same applies to a workspace `.needle.yaml` carrying a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-145-`NON_OVERRIDABLE_KEYS` section. Silence is what let the fleet run for days on a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-146-config file that read as correct.
--
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-148-### 7. Secrets are re-resolved, never re-logged, and never tear down a live exporter
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-149-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-150-`env:`-prefixed header values are re-resolved from the process environment on a
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:151:Tier-B telemetry rebuild. A reload **cannot** introduce a new environment
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-152-variable into a running process, so if a rebuild would need a header the process
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-153-does not have, the existing exporter is kept and the condition reported. A
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md:154:reload must never convert a working exporter into a dead one. Header values are
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-155-never written to a log, an event, or an error message.
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-156-
./docs/adr/017-configuration-hot-reload-at-the-cycle-boundary.md-157-## Consequences
--

### New Component Map (Phase 4)

| Component | Responsibility | Inputs | Outputs |
|-----------|---------------|--------|---------|
| **TraceCapture** | Capture full execution traces from agent runs | Agent stdout/stderr, tool call logs | Structured trace files (JSONL) |
| **Retrospective** | Extract learning from completed beads | Bead close body, execution trace, outcome | Structured retrospective entries |
| **Learnings** | Workspace-scoped knowledge store | Retrospective entries | `learnings.md` updates |
| **Consolidator** | Periodic pattern extraction and pruning | All retrospectives since last run | Updated learnings, CLAUDE.md proposals |
| **SkillLibrary** | Indexed store of proven procedures | Validated learnings, successful patterns | Skill files, context injection |
| **TemplateVersioner** | Track prompt template versions in telemetry | Template content, hash | Versioned telemetry tags |
| **StatsEngine** | Aggregate outcomes by template version, task type, worker | Telemetry JSONL | Success rates, comparisons |

## Trace Capture

### Problem

NEEDLE currently captures exit code, stdout, and stderr from agent processes. This is sufficient for outcome classification but insufficient for learning. To understand *why* a task failed, we need the full execution trace: every tool call, every decision point, every piece of agent reasoning.

### Design

Extend the `dispatch` module to capture structured traces alongside raw output:

```
struct ExecutionResult {
    exit_code: i32,
    stdout: String,
    stderr: String,
    elapsed: Duration,
    pid: u32,
    trace_path: Option<PathBuf>,  // NEW: path to structured trace file
}
```

Trace capture is adapter-specific:

| Agent | Trace Source | Format |
|-------|-------------|--------|
| Claude Code | `--output-format json` or session transcript | Claude JSONL |
| Codex | Agent output stream | OpenAI JSONL |
| Aider | Chat history file | Aider markdown |
| Generic | stdout/stderr passthrough | Raw text |

Traces are stored in `.beads/traces/<bead-id>/`:

```
.beads/traces/nd-a3f8/
  trace.jsonl         # structured tool calls and reasoning
  stdout.txt          # raw stdout
  stderr.txt          # raw stderr
  metadata.json       # timing, tokens, cost, template version
```

### Trace Sanitization

Agent output routinely contains secrets: API keys, database connection strings, auth tokens, environment variables, and credentials. Storing these as plain JSONL in `.beads/traces/` is a data leak. All traces are sanitized before being written to disk.

**Rule source: gitleaks.** Rather than hand-rolling regex patterns, NEEDLE imports the rule database from [gitleaks](https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks) — the industry-standard secret detection tool. Gitleaks ships 222 rules in a single TOML file (`config/gitleaks.toml`) covering 120+ services across cloud providers, AI/ML platforms, CI/CD, payment processors, communication tools, and generic patterns. The regexes use RE2 syntax, which is directly compatible with Rust's `regex` crate.

**Sanitization pipeline (applied to all trace content before write):**

1. **Gitleaks rule set.** NEEDLE vendors a copy of `gitleaks.toml` at build time and parses the `[[rules]]` entries. Each rule provides:
   - `regex` — RE2 pattern matching the secret
   - `keywords` — fast pre-filter strings checked before the expensive regex (Aho-Corasick). If keywords are specified and none are found in the content, the regex is skipped.
   - `secretGroup` — which capture group contains the actual secret (only that portion is redacted, preserving surrounding context)
   - `entropy` — minimum Shannon entropy threshold. Distinguishes real secrets from placeholders like `your-api-key-here`.

   Key rule categories:
   | Category | Examples | Rule Count |
   |----------|----------|------------|
   | Cloud providers | AWS access keys, Azure AD secrets, GCP API keys | ~25 |
   | AI/ML | Anthropic, OpenAI, HuggingFace, Cohere API keys | ~8 |
   | Git/CI/CD | GitHub PATs, GitLab tokens, CircleCI, Drone | ~25 |
   | Communication | Slack (8 token types), Discord, Telegram, Twilio | ~15 |
   | Payment/Finance | Stripe, Plaid, Coinbase, Kraken | ~12 |
   | DevOps/Infra | HashiCorp Vault, Grafana, Datadog, Sentry | ~15 |
   | Generic | `generic-api-key`, JWT, private keys (RSA/EC), curl auth headers | ~10 |
   | Package registries | npm, PyPI, NuGet, RubyGems | ~8 |

   Redaction output includes the rule ID for auditability: `[REDACTED:aws-access-token]`

2. **Custom patterns.** Workspace-specific patterns configured in `.needle.yaml`, applied after the gitleaks rules:
   ```yaml
   learning:
     trace_sanitization:
       custom_patterns:
         - id: "kalshi-api-key"
           regex: "KALSHI_API_KEY=[^\\s]+"
           keywords: ["kalshi"]
         - id: "openbao-token"
           regex: "openbao\\s+token\\s+\\S+"
           keywords: ["openbao"]
       redaction_text: "[REDACTED]"    # default: "[REDACTED:<rule-id>]"
   ```

3. **Gitleaks allowlists and stopwords.** The gitleaks rule set includes per-rule allowlists (regexes, paths, stopwords) and ~1,480 global stopwords that suppress common false positives (e.g., `example`, `test`, `placeholder`). NEEDLE applies these to reduce over-redaction.

4. **Known-safe passthrough.** Structured fields that are never secrets (bead IDs, file paths, exit codes, timestamps, tool names) bypass sanitization for performance.

**Implementation approach:**

- **Option A (preferred): Vendor the TOML.** Include `gitleaks.toml` as a build-time asset. Parse it with `toml` crate into rule structs. Apply keyword pre-filter (Aho-Corasick via `aho-corasick` crate), then regex, then entropy check. This gives full control, no runtime dependency, and works offline.
- **Option B: Shell out to gitleaks.** Pipe trace content through `gitleaks stdin --no-git`. Simpler but adds a runtime dependency and process overhead per trace file.

Option A is preferred because trace sanitization is on the critical write path — it must be fast and cannot fail due to a missing binary.

**Updating the rule set:** `gitleaks.toml` is vendored, not dynamically fetched. To update, run `needle update-rules` which downloads the latest `config/gitleaks.toml` from the gitleaks repository and rebuilds. Rule updates are versioned in NEEDLE's git history.

**Design notes:**
- Sanitization is best-effort, not a security boundary. Traces should still be treated as sensitive and not committed to git or shared externally.
- The redaction is one-way — original content is not recoverable from sanitized traces. If a secret appears in a trace, it is replaced with `[REDACTED:<rule-id>]` and the original is lost.
- Sanitization runs synchronously before the trace file is written. There is no window where unsanitized content exists on disk.
- Over-redaction is preferred to under-redaction. A false positive (redacting a non-secret that looks like a key) is acceptable; a false negative (leaking a real key) is not.
- The keyword pre-filter is critical for performance. Without it, running 222 regexes against every line of trace output would be prohibitive. Keywords reduce the candidate set to ~5-10 rules per line on average.

### Trace Retention

Traces are large. Retention policy:

- **Failed beads:** Keep traces for 30 days (needed for failure analysis)
- **Successful beads:** Keep metadata.json only, delete trace.jsonl after 7 days
- **Configurable:** `learning.trace_retention_days` in `.needle.yaml`

## Bead Retrospectives

### Integration with Pluck Template

The pluck template is extended with retrospective instructions. Before closing a bead, the agent writes a structured learning block:

```
## Retrospective
- **What worked:** [approach that succeeded]
- **What didn't:** [approach that failed and why]
- **Surprise:** [anything unexpected about the codebase/tooling]
- **Reusable pattern:** [if this task type recurs, do X]
```

This block is included in the `br close --body` content. It lives in the JSONL log alongside all other bead data.

### Retrospective Extraction

The consolidator reads bead close bodies and extracts retrospective blocks into structured data:

```json
{
  "bead_id": "nd-a3f8",
  "worker_id": "needle-claude-anthropic-sonnet-alpha",
  "timestamp": "2026-04-04T15:30:00Z",
  "task_type": "bug-fix",
  "what_worked": "...",
  "what_didnt": "...",
  "surprise": "...",
  "reusable_pattern": "..."
}
```

## Workspace Learnings File

### Structure

Each workspace has a `.beads/learnings.md` loaded into all prompts via `context_files`:

```yaml
# .needle.yaml
prompt:
  context_files:
    - CLAUDE.md
    - plan.md
    - .beads/learnings.md    # <-- injected automatically when present
```

### Entry Format

```markdown
### 2026-04-04 | bead: nd-a3f8 | worker: alpha | type: bug-fix
- **Observation:** The kalshi API rate-limits at 5 req/s, not 10 as documented
- **Confidence:** high (verified empirically)
- **Source:** retrospective from bead nd-a3f8
```

### Size Management

- Maximum 80 active entries (configurable: `learning.max_learnings`)
- When exceeded, the consolidator runs automatically
- Entries older than 90 days without reinforcement are pruned

docs/plan/plan.md:4267:# Phase 9: Unify GitHub-Release Upgrade with the Canary-Gated Hot-Reload Channel
docs/plan/plan.md-4268-
docs/plan/plan.md-4269-**Status:** accepted, not yet implemented. [ADR-005](../adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md) was **accepted 2026-08-12**; Phase 9 is authorized work. Two facts from the acceptance re-verification change the implementation and are normative here: the version comparison must be **strictly greater**, never merely different — as of 2026-08-12 the installed binary (`0.2.19`) is *three releases ahead* of the latest published release (`v0.2.16`), so a "versions differ, fetch latest" implementation would silently downgrade the whole fleet through the `:testing` slot; and `hot_reload: true` is already live in `.needle.yaml` with `~/.needle/bin/needle-stable` present, so anything that writes `needle-stable` takes effect fleet-wide **today**, without a canary. Sequence this ahead of ADR-013's staged rollout — Phase 9 is the mechanism that makes that rollout survivable.
docs/plan/plan.md-4270-
docs/plan/plan.md-4271-**Goal:** make a new GitHub release reach every live worker on every fleet host automatically, canary-validated, without a human having to remember to SSH into each host and run `needle upgrade`. Driven by a 2026-07-20 finding during the fleet-wide deployed-artifact improvement review: this repo already ships two binary-update mechanisms — the manual, canary-free `needle upgrade` CLI command, and a fully automatic, already-tested, canary-gated hot-reload pipeline that runs inside every worker's own loop every cycle — but the two are structurally disconnected. The automatic pipeline only ever reacts to a `needle-testing` binary written by the (currently disabled) self-modification pipeline; nothing writes a `needle-testing` binary from a GitHub release. Confirmed live on the ex44 host during this review: the installed `needle` binary reported version `0.2.11` while GitHub's latest published release was `v0.2.12`, published the same day — a real, present instance of the gap, not a hypothetical. Full evidence and rationale in [ADR-005](../adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md).

docs/plan/plan.md:4375:# Phase 11: Deploy-Path Hardening — Hot-Reload Self-Healing and Spawn-Path Guardrails
docs/plan/plan.md-4376-
docs/plan/plan.md-4377-**Status:** planned (ADR-007).
docs/plan/plan.md-4378-
docs/plan/plan.md-4379-**Goal:** make NEEDLE's own binary-replacement paths recover from the two ways they've actually failed in production, rather than relying on an operator remembering the sanctioned procedure every time. Driven by two incidents: 2026-04-30 (`cp`-ing a new binary onto the live spawn path forced an unwanted hash-mismatch re-exec, disrupting 20 sessions) and 2026-07-20 (`mv`-replacing the spawn path instead left running workers hashing a deleted inode — `file_hash` errors every cycle, `check_hot_reload()` logs a warning and continues, and those workers can never hot-reload again). Both share a root cause: NEEDLE's sanctioned deploy path (upgrade → canary → promote → hot-reload) works, but nothing prevents writing directly to the spawn-path binary instead, and the hot-reload check has no recovery behavior for the specific failure a direct `mv` produces. Separately, `needle upgrade` itself still overwrites `env::current_exe()` directly with zero canary validation — the same unvalidated-overwrite shape, just invoked deliberately instead of by accident. Full evidence and rationale in [ADR-007](../adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md).

docs/plan/plan.md-4710-## Decisions locked
docs/plan/plan.md-4711-
docs/plan/plan.md-4712-An implementer will hit each of these forks; the plan decides them so no mid-build ADR is needed.
docs/plan/plan.md-4713-
docs/plan/plan.md:4714:- **Trigger = polled mtime+hash at the cycle boundary**, gated by `worker.config_reload_check_interval_secs` (`0` = disabled, and is the default until the feature has fleet time). *Because* it mirrors `check_hot_reload`'s existing binary-hash check and adds no dependency. *Rejected:* a `notify` file watcher (new dep, new async task, and it must defer to the boundary anyway). *Enforced by* 18.2.
docs/plan/plan.md-4715-- **SIGHUP must NOT be the trigger.** *Because* `install_unix_signal_handlers` already registers `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT`/`SIGHUP` onto the shutdown flag, deliberately — a killed tmux session delivers SIGHUP, and the handler exists so the worker can release its bead and emit `worker.stopped` rather than die silently. Repurposing it turns every tmux teardown into a reload. *Revisit if* the shutdown handler ever stops covering SIGHUP.
docs/plan/plan.md:4716:- **Reload applies only between dispatch cycles.** *Because* a worker in `Building`/`Dispatching`/`Executing`/`Handling` holds a bead, and changing `agent.timeout` or an adapter under a running dispatch produces an outcome attributable to neither config. *Enforced by* 18.2 + the test in 18.8.
docs/plan/plan.md-4717-- **Three declared tiers, written as a table in code, not inferred.** *Because* an untiered atomic swap reads as though everything is reloadable and would silently no-op for immutable keys — recreating the `NON_OVERRIDABLE_KEYS` failure that caused this work. *Enforced by* 18.1.
docs/plan/plan.md-4718-- **Validate before swap; a reload may never fail closed.** *Because* polled reload reaches every worker within one interval, so a bad edit is fleet-wide and simultaneous — strictly worse than the restart-only status quo unless validation is a hard gate. A config problem must degrade telemetry, never remove a worker. *Enforced by* 18.3.
docs/plan/plan.md-4719-- **The `reload::Layer` seam is installed unconditionally at boot, including when OTLP is off.** *Because* `try_init()` is one-shot; installing the seam only when OTLP is already enabled means turning OTLP *on* still needs a restart — the exact situation this phase exists to remove, undiscoverable until tried on a live fleet. *Enforced by* 18.5.
docs/plan/plan.md-4720-- **A reload never tears down a working exporter.** *Because* a reload cannot introduce a new environment variable into a running process, so a rebuild needing an absent `env:` header must keep the old exporter and report. *Enforced by* 18.5.

docs/plan/plan.md-4729-- **Normative rule:** a new config key must be assigned a tier in the same change that introduces it. An unclassified key is a compile-time failure, not a runtime surprise.

docs/configuration.md-30-
docs/configuration.md:31:`worker.config_reload_check_interval_secs` controls how often a worker checks
docs/configuration.md-32-for configuration changes at the cycle boundary, between dispatches. A value
docs/configuration.md-33-of `0` disables the check and is the default until the feature has had fleet
docs/configuration.md-34-time:
docs/configuration.md-35-

docs/configuration.md-39-```
docs/configuration.md-40-
docs/configuration.md:41:This setting is Tier C (restart-required) because it gates the reload mechanism
docs/configuration.md-42-itself. In particular, a worker started with the default `0` cannot discover a
docs/configuration.md-43-file change that enables polling; restart the worker after changing this value.

docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md:1:# Isolated OTLP wire capture
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-2-
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md:3:Use `scripts/otlp-wire-capture.sh` when an OTLP problem must be checked at the
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-4-socket boundary—for example, when NEEDLE and the collector are healthy but
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-5-resource attributes are missing in the backend. It starts a receiver on
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-6-`127.0.0.1` only, captures the raw HTTP/protobuf POST bodies, and returns an
--
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-20-- `otlp-payloads.strings`: printable strings extracted from that binary;
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-21-- `needle.log`: the probe worker's output.
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-22-
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md:23:The copied config is sanitized before it is written to scratch. OTLP is forced
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-24-to local HTTP with no compression, `headers: []`, and no Authorization header;
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-25-the script then scans the generated file and refuses to run if any
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-26-case-insensitive `Authorization:` remains. The probe runs with a scrubbed
--
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-38-Look for resource keys such as `service.name`, `service.version`,
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-39-`service.instance.id`, and the `needle.*` attributes. The worker normally exits
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md-40-nonzero when the empty probe repository has no bead backend; that is expected.
docs/notes/otlp-wire-capture.md:41:The harness succeeds only when at least one OTLP body was captured.

docs/notes/operational-fleet-lessons.md-98-versions to running workers. While useful for rapid iteration, it amplified

docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-6-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-7-## Context
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-8-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:9:Found during a plan.md maturity review of NEEDLE's own deploy history. Two separate incidents, both involving the same underlying mechanism, neither addressed by ADR-005 (Phase 9), which solves a different problem in the same area (getting a *new* GitHub release into the `:testing` slot automatically — not making the existing hash-based hot-reload check resilient to how binaries actually get replaced on disk today).
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-10-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:11:NEEDLE's worker hot-reload (`check_hot_reload()`, `src/upgrade/mod.rs:360`, run every worker-loop cycle at a safe boundary) compares `file_hash(get_current_binary_path())` against `file_hash(~/.needle/bin/needle-stable)`. `get_current_binary_path()` (`src/upgrade/mod.rs:311-313`) does call `env::current_exe()` fresh each cycle — it is not cached or stale by construction. The problem is what happens when that fresh call resolves to a path Linux has already unlinked:
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-12-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:13:- **2026-04-30 (`cp` incident, ex44):** installing a new build via `cp NEEDLE/target/release/needle ~/.local/bin/needle` while 20 workers were running caused every one of them to detect a hash mismatch against the (now-different-content) spawn-path file and re-exec into `:stable` immediately — losing whatever the new build was intended to deliver and disrupting all 20 sessions at once. `cp` overwrites file *content* in place; every running worker's `env::current_exe()` still resolves to a valid, readable path, so the hash check works exactly as designed — it just wasn't the deploy the operator wanted, because the spawn path was written to directly instead of routing through `:testing`.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:14:- **2026-07-20 (`mv` incident, v0.2.12 rollout):** `mv`-replacing the spawn-path binary (`~/.local/bin/needle`) instead of `cp`-ing over it was expected to avoid the previous incident's disruption. It produced a *worse*, silent failure: `mv` unlinks the old directory entry and creates a new one, so any process still holding the old inode open (every running worker) now has `/proc/self/exe` resolving to `"…/needle (deleted)"`. `file_hash()` (`src/upgrade/mod.rs:320-327`) does `fs::read()` on that literal string, which errors every time. `Worker::check_hot_reload()` (`src/worker/mod.rs:2413-2416`) catches that error with `tracing::warn!("hot-reload check failed, continuing")` and returns `Ok(())` — the worker carries on, but has now permanently lost the ability to ever hot-reload again, since the next cycle's `env::current_exe()` resolves to the same deleted path and errors the same way. No special-casing of a deleted/unlinked exe exists anywhere in `src/upgrade` or `src/canary` — confirmed by direct read, not inferred. Affected workers stay on the pre-rollout version until they exit naturally (idle timeout, crash, or manual stop); only freshly-launched workers pick up the new binary.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-15-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:16:Both incidents share a root cause: **the sanctioned deploy path (`needle upgrade` → canary → promote to `:stable` → workers hot-reload from `:stable`) exists and works, but nothing prevents an operator from writing directly to the spawn-path binary instead, and the hot-reload check has no recovery behavior for the specific failure mode that direct-write produces.** Plan.md documents the correct procedure in prose ("Never overwrite a running binary in place... verify with `needle status`", `docs/plan/plan.md:4034`) — this is advisory only, not enforced by any code path, and evidently was not sufficient to prevent either incident from a careful operator.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-17-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:18:Separately, and orthogonal to hot-reload: `perform_upgrade()` (`needle upgrade`'s implementation, `src/upgrade/mod.rs:216-308`) still `fs::rename`s a freshly-downloaded GitHub release directly over `env::current_exe()` (lines 280-281) with **zero canary validation** — the same unvalidated-overwrite shape as the incidents above, just invoked deliberately via a documented command instead of by accident via `cp`/`mv`. ADR-005's own Decision explicitly leaves `perform_upgrade()` unchanged (Decision #4: "`needle upgrade` / `perform_upgrade()` remains available unchanged") and its Alternative #1 acknowledges canary-validating it is "worth doing as a small independent hardening regardless... filed as a separate, smaller bead" — but no such bead or phase exists in this repo today. `needle upgrade` is, structurally, a third way to reproduce the same class of incident, just with better UX around it (a real command instead of a raw filesystem operation).
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-19-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-20-## Decision
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-21-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:22:1. **11.1 — Self-heal the deleted-inode case in hot-reload, instead of erroring silently forever.** When `get_current_binary_path()`'s resolved path indicates a deleted/unlinked exe (Linux appends `" (deleted)"` to `/proc/self/exe`'s readlink target; alternatively detect via `fs::read()`'s `ErrorKind::NotFound` on that specific path), treat this as an **unconditional signal to re-exec against `needle-stable` immediately** — not a warning to log and continue past. The reasoning: a worker whose own spawn-path binary has been unlinked is, by definition, running on a file that no longer exists on disk (an orphaned inode, kept alive only by the open file handle) — there is no future in which continuing to run it is better than reloading, and no legitimate scenario where the correct response is "keep running the deleted version forever." This turns the exact 2026-07-20 failure mode into a one-cycle hot-reload instead of a permanent stall, without requiring the operator to have done anything differently.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-23-2. **11.2 — Route `needle upgrade` through the existing `:testing`→canary→`:stable` pipeline instead of a raw overwrite.** `perform_upgrade()` should write the downloaded release to `~/.needle/bin/needle-testing` (reusing 9.1's target path from Phase 9, if landed first — otherwise this phase adds the equivalent standalone) and invoke the existing canary validation before promotion, rather than `fs::rename`-ing directly over `env::current_exe()`. This closes ADR-005's explicitly deferred item rather than leaving it as an unfiled "someday." `needle upgrade`'s UX (a single command an operator runs) is unchanged; what changes is that it no longer bypasses validation just because it was invoked manually.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:24:3. **11.3 — A guardrail that makes unsanctioned spawn-path writes visible, since they can't be fully prevented.** Add a check (in `needle doctor`, and/or a startup check each worker already performs during BOOTING) that detects "this process's own binary file has been modified/replaced since the process started" — e.g. compare a hash or inode+mtime recorded at boot time against the current state of the same path — and distinguish this from a legitimate hot-reload (which re-execs into a *new process*, so the check only needs to catch the in-place-content-changed case a `cp` produces, where the process keeps running without re-exec). Surface it loudly: a `spawn_path.modified_in_place` telemetry event and a `needle doctor` warning naming the affected workers. This doesn't stop an operator from running `cp`, but it stops the failure from being silent — both historical incidents were confusing specifically because nothing said "your spawn-path binary just changed under you."
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:25:4. **Document `needle upgrade` as the only sanctioned path for a manual/single-host update, with the reasoning visible at the call site** — add a one-line note to `needle upgrade --help` and to the top of `docs/plan/plan.md`'s Binary Structure section stating plainly that `cp`/`mv` onto `~/.local/bin/needle` or `~/.needle/bin/needle-stable` while any worker is running is unsupported and will either disrupt live sessions (content overwrite) or permanently stall hot-reload until 11.1 ships (rename/unlink) — not just documented in an ADR an operator has to already know to look for.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:26:5. **Ship through the existing release path**: version bump, needle-ci (fmt + clippy + test on iad-ci), GitHub Release, staged canary rollout (`:testing` → `:stable`) — per plan.md's established Deployment convention. 11.1 in particular should be validated with its own canary fixture (kill a running worker's spawn-path inode via `mv` in a test harness, confirm it self-heals within one cycle) before promotion to `:stable`, since it is itself part of the hot-reload machinery it's patching.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-27-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-28-## Consequences
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-29-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-30-- 11.1 fixes the more severe of the two incidents (permanent silent stall) with a small, contained change to one error-handling branch — no new mechanism, just correct handling of a case that was previously treated as "log and ignore."
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-31-- 11.2 means `needle upgrade` gains a canary-validation step it didn't have before — slightly slower (a canary run instead of an immediate swap), and now depends on the canary fixtures being adequate for a full release diff, the same open risk Phase 9's Consequences already flag. Both phases should share validation work here rather than duplicate it if built close together.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-32-- 11.3 cannot prevent an operator from directly overwriting a binary file — Unix file permissions don't distinguish "the sanctioned upgrade tool" from "any process with write access." It converts a silent failure into a visible one, which is the achievable bar here, not full prevention.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:33:- None of 11.1-11.3 change the `cp` incident's actual disruption (a content-changed spawn-path file legitimately looks like a real hot-reload signal, and workers correctly re-exec on it) — that incident's fix is entirely 11.2 + 11.4 (route deploys through `:testing`/canary and document why direct writes are unsupported), not a hot-reload code change.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-34-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-35-## Evidence
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-36-
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:37:- `src/upgrade/mod.rs:311-313` (`get_current_binary_path`), `:320-327` (`file_hash`), `:360` (`check_hot_reload`); `src/worker/mod.rs:2413-2416` (`Worker::check_hot_reload`, warn-and-continue on error).
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-38-- `src/upgrade/mod.rs:216-308`, specifically `:280-281` (`perform_upgrade`, raw `fs::rename` over `env::current_exe()`).
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:39:- ADR-005 (`docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md`) Decision #4 and Alternative #1 (`perform_upgrade()` explicitly left unvalidated, hardening "filed as a separate, smaller bead" that does not exist in this repo).
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md-40-- `docs/plan/plan.md:4034` (advisory-only deploy-safety note, no code enforcement).
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:41:- 2026-04-30 incident: `cp NEEDLE/target/release/needle ~/.local/bin/needle` on ex44 while 20 workers ran; all 20 hot-reloaded into `:stable` (the prior, unintended version) and their sessions were disrupted.
docs/adr/007-deploy-path-hardening.md:42:- 2026-07-20 incident (v0.2.12 rollout): `mv`-replacing the spawn-path binary left running workers' `/proc/self/exe` reading `"…/needle (deleted)"`; `hot-reload check failed, continuing` logged repeatedly with no recovery; affected workers stayed on the pre-rollout version until natural exit.

docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-15-**What happened:** NEEDLE workers were assigned beads to implement NEEDLE
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-16-features (e.g., nd-3b23 "Fix build.sh concatenation"). A worker modified
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-17-`build.sh`, committed, and pushed. The next `needle upgrade` pulled the
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:18:broken build, rebuilt the binary, and deployed it via hot-reload to all
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-19-running workers. All workers immediately failed.
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-20-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-21-**Root cause:** The LLM agent did not validate the build output before
--
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-23-`dist/needle` binary had syntax errors.
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-24-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-25-**Impact:** All workers in the fleet went down simultaneously because
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:26:hot-reload deployed the broken binary to every running worker.
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-27-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-28-## Incident 2: Hot-Reload Amplifying Bad Changes
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-29-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:30:**What happened:** NEEDLE implemented hot-reload (commits ff63d1d, 67dcbff)
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-31--- workers poll the binary's mtime and re-exec themselves when it changes.
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-32-Combined with `needle upgrade` (which pulls latest, rebuilds, and installs),
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-33-a broken commit could propagate to the entire fleet within seconds.
--
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-36-1. Worker A completes a bead that modifies NEEDLE source
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-37-2. Worker A commits and pushes
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-38-3. Another worker (or CI) runs `build.sh` and `needle upgrade --install`
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:39:4. Hot-reload triggers on all running workers
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-40-5. All workers re-exec with the new (potentially broken) binary
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-41-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-42-**Mitigation added:** The build script now runs `bash -n` syntax checking.
--
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-73-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-74-**What happened:** NEEDLE workers were assigned beads to improve NEEDLE's
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-75-prompt templates. A worker modified the prompt that future workers (including
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:76:itself after hot-reload) would receive. A poorly crafted prompt change caused
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-77-subsequent workers to produce lower-quality output, which created more beads
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-78-to fix the prompt, which produced more poor changes...
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-79-
--
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-105-### 1. Self-modification must be gated
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-106-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-107-NEEDLE should never automatically deploy changes to itself without human
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:108:approval. Hot-reload of the orchestration binary is too dangerous when
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-109-workers can modify the source. Options:
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-110-- Require a human-approved release process for NEEDLE changes
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-111-- Pin workers to a specific NEEDLE version during a work session
--
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-149-### 6. Version immutability during sessions
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-150-
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-151-Once a NEEDLE worker starts, it should run the same version for the duration
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md:152:of its session. Hot-reload was a net negative because it propagated errors
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-153-faster than humans could intervene. Workers should check for updates between
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-154-sessions (between bead completions), not mid-execution.
docs/notes/self-modification-risks.md-155-

docs/adr/016-otlp-resource-propagation-and-roaming-worker-identity.md:1:# ADR-016: OTLP Resource Propagation and Roaming-Worker Identity
docs/adr/016-otlp-resource-propagation-and-roaming-worker-identity.md-2-
docs/adr/016-otlp-resource-propagation-and-roaming-worker-identity.md-3-**Status:** Proposed — 2026-08-16
docs/adr/016-otlp-resource-propagation-and-roaming-worker-identity.md-4-**Deciders:** operator (jedarden)
--
docs/adr/016-otlp-resource-propagation-and-roaming-worker-identity.md-18-(`/api/dashboard` via the `needle-feed` Traefik entrypoint):

docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-15-| Path | Role |

docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-17-| `~/.local/bin/needle` | What `needle run` resolves off `$PATH`. **This is what the fleet launches.** |
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md:18:| `~/.needle/bin/needle-stable` | What hot-reload compares the running binary against. `~/.needle/bin/needle` is a symlink to it. |
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-19-
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-20-They are ordinarily byte-identical copies, but nothing enforces that. **Restore
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md:21:both**, or you will roll back the fleet's launcher while hot-reload still points
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-22-at the bad build (or the reverse).

docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md:76:binary makes `/proc/self/exe` report `... (deleted)`, which hot-reload treats as
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-77-a forced re-exec (`HotReloadCheck::CurrentBinaryDeleted`).

docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-108-## Notes
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-109-
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-110-- The canary and the fleet can run different NEEDLE versions at the same time.
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md:111:  Nothing reconciles them today, because `Worker::check_hot_reload` is never
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-112-  called (`#[allow(dead_code)]`) and `self_modification.enabled` is `false`.
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md:113:  **If hot-reload is ever re-enabled this stops being true**: it compares the
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-114-  running binary's *hash* against `needle-stable` and re-execs into
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-115-  `needle-stable` on any difference — hashes, not versions — so a worker
docs/notes/needle-binary-rollback.md-116-  deliberately pinned to a newer build gets dragged back.

docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-6-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-7-## Context
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-8-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:9:NEEDLE ships two binary-update mechanisms that share vocabulary (`:testing` / `:stable`, "canary", "hot-reload") but are structurally disconnected — verified by direct code read, not assumption:
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-10-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-11-1. **Manual GitHub-release upgrade** — `needle upgrade` (`perform_upgrade()`, `src/upgrade/mod.rs`). Calls `check_for_update()` against the GitHub Releases API, downloads the new binary, and `fs::rename`s it directly over `env::current_exe()` — in place, on whichever host and path the operator happens to be running it from. No canary validation of any kind. No fleet propagation: every host needs a human to run this individually, and nothing ever does so automatically.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-12-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:13:2. **Self-modification canary/hot-reload channel** — `src/canary/mod.rs` plus `check_auto_canary()` / `check_hot_reload()` in `src/worker/mod.rs`. This is a real, already-implemented, already-tested automatic pipeline: every worker's own loop, on every cycle, between the LOGGING and SELECTING states —
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-14-   - `check_auto_canary()`: if `~/.needle/bin/needle-testing` exists, runs the canary suite against it in the `~/.needle/canary/` workspace; on an all-pass report, calls `runner.promote()`, which moves `needle-testing` → `needle-stable` (backing up the previous `needle-stable` → `needle-stable.prev` for rollback);
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:15:   - `check_hot_reload()`: independently, on every cycle, hashes the currently-running binary against `~/.needle/bin/needle-stable`; on a mismatch, calls `re_exec_stable()`, which `exec()`s into the new binary with `--resume --identifier <worker_name>`, preserving worker identity, at a safe loop boundary (never mid-dispatch).
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-16-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-17-   This machinery works and is unit-tested (`promote_moves_testing_to_stable`, `promote_first_time_no_existing_stable`, etc. in `src/canary/mod.rs`). But `check_auto_canary()` is gated behind `self.config.self_modification.enabled && self.config.self_modification.auto_promote` — both `false` in the live fleet config (`.needle.yaml`: `self_modification: { enabled: false, auto_promote: false, ... }`) — and, independent of that gate, **nothing in the codebase ever writes a `needle-testing` binary from a GitHub release.** The only producer of `needle-testing` today is the (currently disabled) self-modification pipeline, where an agent edits NEEDLE's own source, builds it locally, and drops the result at that path. `check_for_update()` / `perform_upgrade()` never touch `~/.needle/bin/` at all.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-18-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:19:**Live evidence, gathered during this audit (2026-07-20, ex44 host):**
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-20-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-21-```
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-22-$ needle --version
--
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-26-2026-07-20T12:49:30Z
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-27-```
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-28-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:29:The installed binary on the very host running this audit is one release behind, published the same day. Neither existing update path would ever notice or correct this on its own: `needle upgrade` requires a human to run it; the canary/hot-reload channel is structurally blind to GitHub releases. `ps aux` and `needle list`/`needle status` at the time of this audit showed zero live NEEDLE processes on ex44 (fleet activity currently concentrated elsewhere), so there was no live worker mid-loop to observe skipping the check — but the same gap applies whether or not a worker happens to be running at any given moment, since neither path is wired to react to a new release regardless.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-30-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:31:The fleet runs `worker.max_workers: 10` per host across at least two hosts (ex44, lab per CLAUDE.md), each worker a long-lived, independent tmux-session loop (README: "Independence — each worker is a self-contained loop in its own tmux session," "no central orchestrator"). Any fix has to preserve that no-central-orchestrator property rather than introduce a controller that pushes upgrades to hosts.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-32-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-33-## Decision
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-34-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:35:1. **Route GitHub releases through the existing `:testing` slot, not a new path.** Add a download step that fetches a new GitHub release (reusing `check_for_update()`'s version-comparison logic) and writes it to `~/.needle/bin/needle-testing` — the same drop point the self-modification pipeline already uses — instead of `perform_upgrade()`'s current direct in-place overwrite. This means zero new promotion or propagation code: `check_auto_canary()` and `check_hot_reload()`, already running in every worker's loop today, pick up a release-sourced `:testing` binary exactly as they would a self-modification-sourced one.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-36-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-37-2. **Own the periodic check in `needle supervise`, not in the worker loop.** The supervisor daemon already runs continuously per host, independent of bead dispatch, and already makes fleet-wide operational decisions (auto-scaling by queue depth). Add a configurable poll (`supervisor.update_check_interval_secs`, default 6h — infrequent enough to stay well clear of GitHub API rate limits across a multi-host fleet, frequent enough that drift like the 0.2.11/0.2.12 case above self-corrects same-day) that calls `check_for_update()` and, if newer, downloads to `needle-testing`.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-38-
--
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-44-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-45-## Alternatives Considered
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-46-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:47:1. **Canary-validate inside `perform_upgrade()` itself, keep it manual-only.** Rejected as the primary fix: still requires a human to remember to run `needle upgrade` on every host, which is exactly the condition that produced the observed drift (nobody ran it on ex44 despite the release being hours old). Worth doing as a minor independent hardening (today, even the manual path installs with zero validation) — filed as a separate, smaller bead rather than folded into this decision.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-48-2. **Central push** — a control host SSHes into every fleet host and runs `needle upgrade`. Rejected: reintroduces the single controller NEEDLE's own design explicitly avoids (README: "no central orchestrator... coordination happens through the shared bead queue"), and no host-inventory/SSH-fanout tooling exists for the fleet today — this would be new infrastructure bolted on sideways rather than reuse of what's already built.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-49-3. **Check on every worker-loop iteration instead of via the supervisor.** Rejected: couples a GitHub API call and a binary download to bead-dispatch latency, and roaming/short-lived workers (Explore strand) don't have a reliable idle moment to safely absorb that cost in. The supervisor already exists specifically as the per-host, dispatch-independent decision-maker; this is what it's for.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-50-4. **Do nothing automatic — just have `needle status` print "N releases behind."** Rejected as a complete fix (a human still has to notice and act, per host, per release — the same failure mode observed), but cheap enough to ship immediately as a stopgap while the real mechanism lands; filed separately.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-51-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-52-## Consequences
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-53-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:54:- **Positive:** Closes the exact drift class observed live during this audit without inventing a new, weaker "just overwrite the binary" auto-update mechanism — it reuses the canary gate, promotion, and hot-reload-at-safe-boundary machinery that already exists, is already unit-tested, and already has a rollback story (`needle rollback`).
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-55-- **Positive:** Preserves the no-central-orchestrator design principle — every host polls GitHub and canary-validates independently against its own local canary workspace; no host's upgrade depends on any other host or on a controller.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-56-- **Risk:** The canary suite's existing fixtures were designed and tuned against agent-authored source-level self-modifications. It is not yet confirmed those same fixtures give adequate coverage for a full official-release binary swap, which can legitimately change more surface at once (new CLI flags, new default adapters, schema changes). Needs its own validation pass — ideally a canary scenario built specifically from a real release diff — before `auto_upgrade_check` defaults on fleet-wide.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-57-- **Risk:** Two producers can now write `~/.needle/bin/needle-testing` — the self-modification pipeline and the new supervisor-driven release check. Needs a simple mutual-exclusion rule (e.g., the supervisor's release check skips writing if a `:testing` binary is already present and unpromoted, so it never clobbers an in-flight self-modification candidate mid-validation).
--
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-61-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-62-- `src/upgrade/mod.rs`: `perform_upgrade()` (GitHub-release download, direct overwrite of `env::current_exe()`, no canary step) and `check_for_update()` (version comparison against GitHub Releases API) — read directly.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-63-- `src/canary/mod.rs`: release-channel doc comment (`needle-testing` / `needle-stable` / `needle-stable.prev`), `CanaryRunner::testing_binary()` / `stable_binary()` / `promote()` — read directly; unit tests `promote_moves_testing_to_stable`, `promote_first_time_no_existing_stable`, `promote_no_testing_binary_fails` confirm the promotion mechanics are implemented and tested.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:64:- `src/worker/mod.rs` (~lines 2260–2410): `check_auto_canary()` and `check_hot_reload()`, called every worker-loop cycle between LOGGING and SELECTING; gating on `self_modification.enabled` / `auto_promote` / `hot_reload` confirmed by direct read.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:65:- `.needle.yaml` (this repo's live, self-hosted config): `self_modification: { enabled: false, auto_promote: false, hot_reload: true, ... }` — confirms the propagation half is armed (`hot_reload: true`) but the trigger half is not.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:66:- Live version check, 2026-07-20, ex44 host: `needle --version` → `needle 0.2.11`; GitHub API `releases/latest` → `v0.2.12`, `published_at: 2026-07-20T12:49:30Z` (same day).
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:67:- `needle status` / `needle list` / `ps aux | grep needle` at time of audit: zero live NEEDLE-prefixed processes on ex44 (fleet activity elsewhere) — confirms the gap is structural (no path reacts to a new release), not merely "a worker happened to not check yet."
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-68-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-69-## Addendum (2026-08-12): acceptance re-verification — the drift direction inverted
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-70-
--
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-72-problem this ADR exists to solve is still real, but it now points the other way,
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-73-and that changes one implementation detail materially.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-74-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:75:**Then (2026-07-20, ex44):** installed `needle 0.2.11`, latest GitHub release
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-76-`v0.2.12`. The host lagged the release by one.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-77-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:78:**Now (2026-08-12, ex44):** installed `needle 0.2.19`, latest GitHub release
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-79-`v0.2.16`. The host is **three releases ahead of anything published.**
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-80-
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-81-So the failure mode is no longer "nobody ran `needle upgrade`" — it is that the
--
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-88-`check_for_update()` must compare **strictly greater**, never merely different.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-89-With today's state a "versions differ, fetch latest" implementation would
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-90-*downgrade* the fleet from 0.2.19 to 0.2.16 on the first poll, and — because the
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:91:Decision routes releases through `:testing` where `check_hot_reload()` will pick
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-92-them up — it would do so automatically, on every host, at the next canary
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-93-promotion. A downgrade is indistinguishable from an upgrade to every mechanism
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-94-downstream of the version comparison, so the comparison is the only place it can
--
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-102-- `.needle.yaml` still has `self_modification.enabled: false` and
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-103-  `auto_promote: false`, exactly as the Context describes, so the canary gate is
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-104-  still inert.
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:105:- **`hot_reload: true` is set and the channel is live**, which the Context did not
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md:106:  state. `~/.needle/bin/needle-stable` exists, so `check_hot_reload()` runs its
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-107-  hash comparison on every worker cycle rather than short-circuiting on a missing
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-108-  `:stable`. It is currently quiescent only because the running binary and
docs/adr/005-unify-release-upgrade-with-canary-hot-reload.md-109-  `needle-stable` are byte-identical (both `sha256:9ce8450c…`, 0.2.19). The
--
